- 4 = spontaneous
- 3 = to speech
- 2 = to pain - 1 = none

2. **Verbal Response (V Verbal):**
- 5 = oriented
- 4 = confused - 3 = words, but not coherent
- 2 = sounds only
- 1 = none

3. **Motor Response ( M Motor):**
- 6 = obeys commands
- 5 = localizing pain
- 4 = flexion (withdrawal) to pain
- 3 = abnormal flexion (decorticate) to pain
- 2 = abnormal extension (decerebrate) to pain
- 1 = no response to pain

- Heart rate = 7 2 bpm 
- QT interval = 330 msec

First, I have to remember that the RR interval is the time between two consecutive R waves in an Electrocardiogram (ECG), and it corresponds to the heart rate. Since the heart rate is given in beats per minute ( bpm), we can convert it to the RR interval in seconds. The formula to convert heart rate (HR) to RR interval (in seconds) is RR = 60 / HR. So, let 's calculate the RR interval:

\[ \text{RR interval} = \ frac{60}{\text{Heart rate}} = \frac{ 60}{72} \]

Using a calculator,

\[ \sqrt{0 .8 333} \approx 0.9 1 3. \]

Now we use the Bazett formula:

The corrected QT interval (QTc) is calculated by dividing the measured QT interval by the square root of the RR interval:

\[ \text{QTc} = \frac{ QT }{ \sqrt{RR} } \]

Filtered text: The common formula used for calculating body surface area is the Mosteller formula: \[ \text{BSA (m^2)} = \sqrt {\frac{\text{(height (cm) \times weight (kg)) }}{3600}} \]

From the patient note, the height is 150 cm. The weight is listed as 3 2 (though it is not specified if it's in kilograms or pounds). The note mentions "weight 32 <5th percentile," so assuming the weight is given in kilograms (since in typical clinical settings, weight in children is often recorded in kg), the weight would be 32 kg . Now we will plug in the height and weight into the Mosteller formula :

Height = 150 cm,
Weight = 32 kg ,

BSA = sqrt( (150 * 32) / 3600)

Let's calculate this step by step: Step 1: Calculate the product of height and weight. \[ 150 \times 32 = 48 00. \]

Step 2: Divide this product by 3 600. \[ 4800 / 36 00 = 1.3333. \]

Thus, the HEART score is 7. The score helps assess the likelihood of acute coronary syndrome (ACS). Each component is assigned a score from 0 to 3 points, and the total score ranges from 0 to 12. A higher score indicates a higher risk of ACS. The components are:

- **History**: The clinical presentation. - **ECG**: Presence of ischemic changes. - **Age**: The patient's age. - **Risk factors**: Presence of risk factors for ACS. - **Troponin**: Evidence of myocardial injury. Let's go through each component step by step:

### 1. **History**
The patient presented with pleuritic chest pain associated with short ness of breath. The chest pain was pleuritic, which is more suggestive of peric arditis rather than ischemic heart pain. This suggests a lower likelihood of ACS. The HEART history component scores are:
- **0**: No chest pain. - **1**: Atypical chest pain. - ** 2**: Typical chest pain. - **3**: Cardiac arrest. Since the pain is pleuritic (atypical), this would score as 1. ### 2. **Electrocardiogram ( ECG)**
The ECG showed normal sinus rhythm with low voltages in the limb leads; there were no diffuse ST-segment elevations. The absence of ischemic changes (no ST elevations, depressions, or T-wave inversions) would score 0. The key here is whether there are signs of ischemia. Since it mentions no ST changes, I'll score this as 0. ### 3. **Age**
The patient is 47 years old. - 45-64: 2 points. Since he's 47, this would be 2 points. ### 4. **Risk Factors**
The risk factors for ACS generally include : hypertension, diabetes, hyperlipidemia, smoking, family history of CAD, etc. However, in the HEART scoring system, the risk factor component is binary : the presence of any major risk factors (at least 2 major risk factors like HTN, DM, hyperlipidemia, smoking, or family history) scores 1. The patient has hypertension and diabetes, so he has at least two major risk factors. ### 5. **Troponin**
